VTU ENGINEERING: C PROGRAMMING MODULE 2 • 1D Arrays
QUESTION 27: One-Dimensional Arrays: Declaration, Memory Layout, and Indexing
Fundamental
Core Concept Viva Must-Know
A. Problem Statement / Question:
What is a one-dimensional array? Explain array declaration, initialization syntax (complete, partial, zero), 0-based indexing, and contiguous memory allocation with address proof.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. Definition: `data_type array_name[SIZE];` 2. Memory Model: Elements are stored in contiguous memory cells. If element `arr[0]` is at address `0x1000`, and elements are 4-byte integers, then `arr[i]` is strictly at `0x1000 + i * sizeof(int)`. 3. Initialization Variants: - Full: `int a[5] = {10, 20, 30, 40, 50};` - Partial: `int a[5] = {10, 20};` (remaining 3 elements are automatically initialized to 0). - Zero-init: `int a[5] = {0};` (all 5 elements initialized to 0). - Deduced size: `int a[] = {1, 2, 3};` (compiler sets size to 3). 4. Bounds: Index ranges from `0` to `SIZE - 1`. C does NOT perform runtime boundary checking.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

An array is a fixed-size, homogeneous collection of contiguous memory locations storing elements of identical data type under a single identifier.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
type name[size]; Address of element i = BaseAddress + i * sizeof(type)
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void) {
    int scores[5] = {85, 92, 78, 64, 99};

    printf("=== 1D Array Contiguous Memory Map ===\n");
    printf("%-8s | %-6s | %-16s | %-10s\n", "Index", "Value", "Memory Address", "Byte Offset");
    printf("-------------------------------------------------\n");

    uintptr_t baseAddr = (uintptr_t)&scores[0];

    for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
        uintptr_t currentAddr = (uintptr_t)&scores[i];
        printf("scores[%d] | %-6d | %p | +%td bytes\n", 
               i, scores[i], (void*)&scores[i], currentAddr - baseAddr);
    }

    printf("\nTotal Array Size in RAM: %zu bytes (%zu elements * %zu bytes)\n", 
           sizeof(scores), sizeof(scores)/sizeof(scores[0]), sizeof(int));

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
=== 1D Array Contiguous Memory Map ===
Index    | Value  | Memory Address   | Byte Offset
-------------------------------------------------
scores[0] | 85     | 0x7ffd5a68acb0   | +0 bytes
scores[1] | 92     | 0x7ffd5a68acb4   | +4 bytes
scores[2] | 78     | 0x7ffd5a68acb8   | +8 bytes
scores[3] | 64     | 0x7ffd5a68acbc   | +12 bytes
scores[4] | 99     | 0x7ffd5a68acc0   | +16 bytes

Total Array Size in RAM: 20 bytes (5 elements * 4 bytes)
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. Every successive element address increases by exactly `4` bytes (`sizeof(int)`). 2. This contiguous layout allows random access in $O(1)$ constant time via base address pointer arithmetic. 3. `sizeof(scores) / sizeof(scores[0])` calculates array length at compile time.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Declaring an array with empty size and no initializer: `int arr[];`.
✓ How to Avoid: The compiler cannot deduce the memory to allocate. You must either specify the size `int arr[10];` or provide an initializer list `int arr[] = {1, 2};`.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Assuming `scores[5]` accesses the 5th element.
✓ How to Avoid: With 0-based indexing, the 5th element is `scores[4]`. Accessing `scores[5]` is an out-of-bounds array access leading to undefined behavior.

QUESTION 28: Array Operations: Minimum, Maximum, and Average Computation
Fundamental
Lab Program Exam Level
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Write a complete C program to read N integers into a 1D array. Compute and display the Maximum element, Minimum element, Sum, and Arithmetic Average with precision formatting.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. Never initialize `max` with 0 or a hardcoded value if the array might contain negative numbers! Initializing with `arr[0]` guarantees correct results for all numerical ranges. 2. Running Sum: Accumulate in an integer or 64-bit integer. 3. Average: Must cast `(double)sum / n` to avoid integer division truncation.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

Initialize `max` and `min` with `arr[0]`. Iterate through the array starting from index 1. If an element is greater than `max`, update `max`. If an element is smaller than `min`, update `min`. Accumulate values into `sum`.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
if (arr[i] > max) max = arr[i]; if (arr[i] < min) min = arr[i];
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void) {
    int arr[] = {45, -12, 89, 0, 104, -34, 76, 23};
    int n = sizeof(arr) / sizeof(arr[0]);

    int max = arr[0];
    int min = arr[0];
    long long sum = 0;

    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (arr[i] > max) max = arr[i];
        if (arr[i] < min) min = arr[i];
        sum += arr[i];
    }

    double avg = (double)sum / n;

    printf("=== Array Statistical Analysis ===\n");
    printf("Elements: ");
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) printf("%d ", arr[i]);
    printf("\n");

    printf("Total Elements (N): %d\n", n);
    printf("Maximum Element:    %d\n", max);
    printf("Minimum Element:    %d\n", min);
    printf("Cumulative Sum:     %lld\n", sum);
    printf("Arithmetic Average: %.2f\n", avg);

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
=== Array Statistical Analysis ===
Elements: 45 -12 89 0 104 -34 76 23 
Total Elements (N): 8
Maximum Element:    104
Minimum Element:    -34
Cumulative Sum:     291
Arithmetic Average: 36.38
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. `max` and `min` are safely seeded with `arr[0] = 45`. 2. Iteration compares each element; `-34` successfully becomes `min` and `104` becomes `max`. 3. Average is computed as `(double)291 / 8 = 36.375`, formatted to `36.38`.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Initializing `min = 0`.
✓ How to Avoid: If all numbers in the array are positive (e.g., `{10, 20, 30}`), `min` will incorrectly remain `0`.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Integer division in average: `double avg = sum / n;`.
✓ How to Avoid: Divides integers first, losing fractional precision before assignment to double.

VTU ENGINEERING: C PROGRAMMING MODULE 2 • Searching Algorithms
QUESTION 29: Linear Search Algorithm: Logic, Trace, and Complexity Analysis
Fundamental
Lab Program VTU Exam Favorite
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Explain the Linear Search algorithm. Write a complete C program to search for a key element in an unsorted array. Analyze its Best, Worst, and Average time complexities.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

Complexity Analysis: - Best Case: $O(1)$ (Key found at index 0 on the first comparison). - Worst Case: $O(N)$ (Key is at the very last index $N-1$, or not present in the array). - Average Case: $O(N)$ (Key found around the middle, requiring $(N+1)/2$ comparisons). - Space Complexity: $O(1)$ (In-place, requires no auxiliary storage). Advantage: Works on unsorted, random data; requires no preprocessing.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

Compare the search key sequentially against every element in the array from index 0 to N-1. If a match is found, return the index and terminate early. If the end of the array is reached without a match, report element not found.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) if (arr[i] == key) return i;
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>

int linearSearch(const int arr[], int size, int key) {
    for (int i = 0; i < size; i++) {
        if (arr[i] == key) {
            return i; /* Key found: Return 0-based index */
        }
    }
    return -1; /* Key not found */
}

int main(void) {
    int data[] = {64, 34, 25, 12, 22, 11, 90};
    int n = sizeof(data) / sizeof(data[0]);

    int searchKeys[] = {22, 99};
    int numQueries = sizeof(searchKeys) / sizeof(searchKeys[0]);

    printf("Array: ");
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) printf("%d ", data[i]);
    printf("\n\n");

    for (int q = 0; q < numQueries; q++) {
        int target = searchKeys[q];
        int resultIndex = linearSearch(data, n, target);

        if (resultIndex != -1) {
            printf(">> Key %d FOUND at index %d (Position %d)\n", 
                   target, resultIndex, resultIndex + 1);
        } else {
            printf(">> Key %d NOT FOUND in the array\n", target);
        }
    }

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
Array: 64 34 25 12 22 11 90 

>> Key 22 FOUND at index 4 (Position 5)
>> Key 99 NOT FOUND in the array
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. For key `22`, the search checks indices 0, 1, 2, 3 before finding a match at index `4`. 2. For key `99`, all 7 elements are examined with no match, returning `-1`.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Printing 'Not Found' inside the search loop.
✓ How to Avoid: If placed inside the loop, the program prints 'Not Found' on every mismatched iteration before finding the key!
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Not passing array length explicitly to the function.
✓ How to Avoid: Arrays decay to pointers when passed to functions; `sizeof(arr)` inside `linearSearch` gives pointer size (4/8 bytes), not the array length.
I. Similar Practice / Viva Question

(Complete verified answer provided in Appendix A at the end of this book).

VTU ENGINEERING: C PROGRAMMING MODULE 2 • Searching Algorithms
QUESTION 30: Binary Search Algorithm: Divide-and-Conquer with Trace
Intermediate
Lab Program High-Yield Exam
A. Problem Statement / Question:
State the mandatory precondition for Binary Search. Explain the divide-and-conquer strategy. Write a C program avoiding integer overflow in midpoint calculation, and derive its $O(\log_2 N)$ complexity.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. Overflow-Safe Midpoint: Writing `mid = (low + high) / 2` causes integer overflow when `low + high` exceeds $2^{31}-1$. Safe formula: `mid = low + (high - low) / 2`. 2. Recurrence Relation: $T(N) = T(N/2) + O(1)$. By Master Theorem, $T(N) = O(\log_2 N)$. 3. Comparison Count: For 1,000,000 items, linear search needs up to 1,000,000 comparisons, whereas binary search requires at most 20 comparisons ($2^{20} \approx 1.04 \times 10^6$).

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

Precondition: The array MUST be sorted. Binary search compares the target with the middle element. If equal, target is found. If target < mid, search left half; if target > mid, search right half. Each step halves the search space.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>

int binarySearch(const int arr[], int size, int target) {
    int low = 0;
    int high = size - 1;
    int comparisons = 0;

    while (low <= high) {
        comparisons++;
        /* Overflow-safe midpoint computation */
        int mid = low + (high - low) / 2;

        if (arr[mid] == target) {
            printf("  [Target %d matched at mid index %d after %d comparison(s)]\n", 
                   target, mid, comparisons);
            return mid;
        } else if (arr[mid] < target) {
            low = mid + 1; /* Discard left half */
        } else {
            high = mid - 1; /* Discard right half */
        }
    }

    printf("  [Target %d not found after %d comparisons]\n", target, comparisons);
    return -1;
}

int main(void) {
    int sortedData[] = {11, 22, 33, 44, 55, 66, 77, 88, 99};
    int n = sizeof(sortedData) / sizeof(sortedData[0]);

    printf("Sorted Data: ");
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) printf("%d ", sortedData[i]);
    printf("\n\n");

    int idx1 = binarySearch(sortedData, n, 66);
    printf("Search 66 -> Index: %d\n\n", idx1);

    int idx2 = binarySearch(sortedData, n, 25);
    printf("Search 25 -> Index: %d\n", idx2);

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
Sorted Data: 11 22 33 44 55 66 77 88 99 

  [Target 66 matched at mid index 5 after 3 comparison(s)]
Search 66 -> Index: 5

  [Target 25 not found after 4 comparisons]
Search 25 -> Index: -1
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. Search for 66: - Pass 1: low=0, high=8, mid=4 (arr[4]=55). Since 55 < 66, low becomes 5. - Pass 2: low=5, high=8, mid=6 (arr[6]=77). Since 77 > 66, high becomes 5. - Pass 3: low=5, high=5, mid=5 (arr[5]=66). Match found in 3 comparisons! 2. Search for 25 terminates with `low > high`, returning `-1`.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Applying Binary Search on an unsorted array.
✓ How to Avoid: Binary search logic fundamentally relies on order. On unsorted data, it produces false negatives.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Using `low = mid` or `high = mid`.
✓ How to Avoid: Can cause infinite loops when `high - low == 1`. Always use `mid + 1` and `mid - 1`.
I. Similar Practice / Viva Question
Practice Exercise 30: How many comparisons does binary search take in the worst case for an array of 64 elements?
(Complete verified answer provided in Appendix A at the end of this book).

VTU ENGINEERING: C PROGRAMMING MODULE 2 • Sorting Algorithms
QUESTION 31: Bubble Sort Algorithm with Early Termination Optimization
Intermediate
Lab Program VTU Exam Favorite
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Explain the Bubble Sort algorithm. Implement the pass-by-pass comparison logic in C with a swapped flag to achieve $O(N)$ best-case runtime for already sorted arrays.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. In pass $i$ (from 0 to $N-2$), the largest unsorted element settles into position $N-1-i$. Hence the inner loop only needs to run up to $N-1-i$. 2. Optimization Flag: If an entire pass completes without a single swap, the array is already sorted. Setting a flag `swapped = 0` and breaking early improves best-case time from $O(N^2)$ to $O(N)$. 3. Time Complexity: Best: $O(N)$ (optimized), Worst: $O(N^2)$, Average: $O(N^2)$. Space: $O(1)$ (stable sort).

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

Repeatedly steps through the list, compares adjacent elements, and swaps them if they are in the wrong order. Larger elements 'bubble up' to the end of the array with each pass.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
if (arr[j] > arr[j+1]) { swap(&arr[j], &arr[j+1]); swapped = 1; }
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>

void bubbleSort(int arr[], int n) {
    int passes = 0;
    int totalSwaps = 0;

    for (int i = 0; i < n - 1; i++) {
        int swapped = 0; /* Optimization flag */
        passes++;

        for (int j = 0; j < n - 1 - i; j++) {
            if (arr[j] > arr[j + 1]) {
                /* Swap adjacent elements */
                int temp = arr[j];
                arr[j] = arr[j + 1];
                arr[j + 1] = temp;

                swapped = 1;
                totalSwaps++;
            }
        }

        /* If no swaps occurred, array is completely sorted */
        if (!swapped) {
            break;
        }
    }
    printf("Sorting completed in %d passes with %d total swaps.\n", passes, totalSwaps);
}

int main(void) {
    int data[] = {64, 25, 12, 22, 11};
    int n = sizeof(data) / sizeof(data[0]);

    printf("Original Array: ");
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) printf("%d ", data[i]);
    printf("\n");

    bubbleSort(data, n);

    printf("Sorted Array:   ");
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) printf("%d ", data[i]);
    printf("\n");

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
Original Array: 64 25 12 22 11 
Sorting completed in 4 passes with 8 total swaps.
Sorted Array:   11 12 22 25 64
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. Pass 1: 64 bubbles to index 4: `{25, 12, 22, 11, 64}`. 2. Pass 2: 25 bubbles to index 3: `{12, 22, 11, 25, 64}`. 3. Pass 3: 22 bubbles to index 2: `{12, 11, 22, 25, 64}`. 4. Pass 4: 12 bubbles to index 1: `{11, 12, 22, 25, 64}`. 5. Result is completely sorted in ascending order.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Running inner loop to `N-1` instead of `N-1-i`.
✓ How to Avoid: Running to `N-1` wastes CPU cycles comparing elements that are already sorted at the tail of the array.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Missing the temporary variable during swap: `a = b; b = a;`.
✓ How to Avoid: Overwrites `a` with `b`, losing the original value of `a`. Always use a temporary storage variable or XOR swap.
I. Similar Practice / Viva Question
Practice Exercise 31: What is the time complexity of the optimized bubble sort on an array that is already sorted?
(Complete verified answer provided in Appendix A at the end of this book).

VTU ENGINEERING: C PROGRAMMING MODULE 2 • 2D Arrays
QUESTION 32: Two-Dimensional Arrays: Row-Major Memory Mapping and Addressing
Intermediate
Exam Theory Viva Favorite
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Explain 2D array representation in C. Derive the row-major address calculation formula for an element at `A[i][j]` with respect to base address, row count, column count, and element size.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

Row-Major Address Formula: For an array declared as `T arr[ROWS][COLS];`: $$\text{Address}(arr[i][j]) = \text{Base Address} + (i \times \text{COLS} + j) \times \text{sizeof}(T)$$ Where: - $i$ = target row index ($0 \le i < \text{ROWS}$) - $j$ = target column index ($0 \le j < \text{COLS}$) - $\text{COLS}$ = total number of columns - $\text{sizeof}(T)$ = bytes per element (e.g., 4 bytes for `int`). Note: The row dimension `ROWS` is NOT needed to locate an element; only the column count `COLS` is required. This is why when passing 2D arrays to functions, the column dimension must always be specified: `void f(int a[][COLS])`.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

C physically stores 2D arrays in contiguous 1D linear RAM using Row-Major Order (row 0 followed by row 1, etc.).

D. Syntax & Language Rules
Address = Base + (i * COLS + j) * sizeof(element)
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdint.h>

#define ROWS 3
#define COLS 4

int main(void) {
    int matrix[ROWS][COLS] = {
        {10, 11, 12, 13},
        {20, 21, 22, 23},
        {30, 31, 32, 33}
    };

    uintptr_t base = (uintptr_t)&matrix[0][0];

    printf("=== Row-Major Contiguous Address Verification ===\n");
    printf("Base Address (&matrix[0][0]): %p\n\n", (void*)base);

    for (int i = 0; i < ROWS; i++) {
        for (int j = 0; j < COLS; j++) {
            uintptr_t actualAddr = (uintptr_t)&matrix[i][j];
            uintptr_t computedAddr = base + (i * COLS + j) * sizeof(int);

            printf("matrix[%d][%d]=%d | Actual: %p | Computed: %p | %s\n",
                   i, j, matrix[i][j], (void*)actualAddr, (void*)computedAddr,
                   (actualAddr == computedAddr) ? "MATCH" : "MISMATCH");
        }
    }

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output

💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. Every single element computed via `Base + (i * 4 + j) * 4` matches the physical RAM address. 2. When moving from `matrix[0][3]` to `matrix[1][0]`, the address increases by 4 bytes contiguously. 3. Row-major order is the standard storage convention across C and C++.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Writing `void func(int a[][])` in function prototypes.
✓ How to Avoid: The C compiler must know the column width to compute element offsets. You must write `void func(int a[][COLS])`.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Confusing Row-Major (C) with Column-Major (Fortran/MATLAB).
✓ How to Avoid: In C, rows are contiguous. In Fortran/MATLAB, columns are contiguous.

VTU ENGINEERING: C PROGRAMMING MODULE 2 • 2D Arrays
QUESTION 33: Matrix Addition and Subtraction with Dimension Compatibility Checks
Fundamental
Lab Program VTU Exam Favorite
A. Problem Statement / Question:
State the mathematical condition for matrix addition and subtraction. Write a complete C program to validate dimensions and perform matrix addition and subtraction.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. Compatibility Rule: Let matrix $A$ be of order $R_1 \times C_1$ and matrix $B$ be of order $R_2 \times C_2$. Addition is valid if and only if $R_1 = R_2$ and $C_1 = C_2$. 2. Time Complexity: $O(R \times C)$ iterating through nested loops. 3. Result matrix order is also $R_1 \times C_1$.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

Matrices can only be added or subtracted if they share identical dimensions: $R_1 == R_2$ and $C_1 == C_2$. Elements are added element-by-element: $C[i][j] = A[i][j] + B[i][j]$.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
C[i][j] = A[i][j] + B[i][j]; D[i][j] = A[i][j] - B[i][j];
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>

#define MAX 10

void printMatrix(int r, int c, int m[MAX][MAX], const char *name) {
    printf("Matrix %s (%dx%d):\n", name, r, c);
    for (int i = 0; i < r; i++) {
        printf("  [ ");
        for (int j = 0; j < c; j++) {
            printf("%4d ", m[i][j]);
        }
        printf("]\n");
    }
}

int main(void) {
    int r1 = 2, c1 = 3;
    int r2 = 2, c2 = 3;

    int A[MAX][MAX] = {{1, 2, 3}, {4, 5, 6}};
    int B[MAX][MAX] = {{7, 8, 9}, {3, 2, 1}};
    int Sum[MAX][MAX], Diff[MAX][MAX];

    /* Validation Check */
    if (r1 != r2 || c1 != c2) {
        printf("Error: Matrix dimensions incompatible for addition/subtraction!\n");
        return 1;
    }

    /* Computation */
    for (int i = 0; i < r1; i++) {
        for (int j = 0; j < c1; j++) {
            Sum[i][j] = A[i][j] + B[i][j];
            Diff[i][j] = A[i][j] - B[i][j];
        }
    }

    printMatrix(r1, c1, A, "A");
    printMatrix(r2, c2, B, "B");
    printMatrix(r1, c1, Sum, "A + B");
    printMatrix(r1, c1, Diff, "A - B");

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
Matrix A (2x3):
  [    1    2    3 ]
  [    4    5    6 ]
Matrix B (2x3):
  [    7    8    9 ]
  [    3    2    1 ]
Matrix A + B (2x3):
  [    8   10   12 ]
  [    7    7    7 ]
Matrix A - B (2x3):
  [   -6   -6   -6 ]
  [    1    3    5 ]
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. Both matrices have order $2 \times 3$. The dimension check passes. 2. Corresponding elements are added and subtracted across 2 nested loops. 3. Formatted display preserves tabular column alignment.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Proceeding with matrix addition without validating row/col equality.
✓ How to Avoid: Attempting to add matrices with different dimensions is mathematically undefined and causes array out-of-bound errors in C.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Swapping row and column indices: writing `m[j][i]` instead of `m[i][j]`.
✓ How to Avoid: Produces the transpose of the intended matrix.

VTU ENGINEERING: C PROGRAMMING MODULE 2 • 2D Arrays
QUESTION 34: Matrix Multiplication: Algorithm, Compatibility, and 3-Loop Implementation
Intermediate
High Yield Exam VTU Lab Mandatory
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Formulate the mathematical condition for matrix multiplication. Write a complete C program to perform matrix multiplication of two matrices with dimension checks using three nested loops.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. Compatibility: $A_{R_1 \times C_1} \times B_{R_2 \times C_2} \implies C_1 = R_2$. 2. Product Size: $R_1 \times C_2$. 3. Three Nested Loops: - Loop 1 (index $i$ from 0 to $R_1-1$): Selects row of $A$. - Loop 2 (index $j$ from 0 to $C_2-1$): Selects column of $B$. - Loop 3 (index $k$ from 0 to $C_1-1$): Computes the dot product $\sum A[i][k] B[k][j]$. 4. Time Complexity: $O(R_1 \times C_1 \times C_2) = O(N^3)$ for square matrices.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

Multiplication $A \times B$ is defined if and only if the number of columns in $A$ equals the number of rows in $B$ ($C_1 == R_2$). The resulting matrix has dimensions $R_1 \times C_2$, where $C[i][j] = \sum_{k=0}^{C_1-1} A[i][k] \cdot B[k][j]$.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
for(i=0; i<r1; i++) for(j=0; j<c2; j++) { c[i][j]=0; for(k=0; k<c1; k++) c[i][j] += a[i][k]*b[k][j]; }
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>

#define MAX 10

int main(void) {
    int r1 = 2, c1 = 3;
    int A[MAX][MAX] = {
        {1, 2, 3},
        {4, 5, 6}
    };

    int r2 = 3, c2 = 2;
    int B[MAX][MAX] = {
        {7, 8},
        {9, 1},
        {2, 3}
    };

    int Product[MAX][MAX];

    /* Validation Rule */
    if (c1 != r2) {
        printf("Error: Matrix multiplication impossible. Columns of A (%d) != Rows of B (%d)\n", c1, r2);
        return 1;
    }

    /* Matrix Multiplication Algorithm */
    for (int i = 0; i < r1; i++) {
        for (int j = 0; j < c2; j++) {
            Product[i][j] = 0; /* Clear accumulator before dot product */
            for (int k = 0; k < c1; k++) {
                Product[i][j] += A[i][k] * B[k][j];
            }
        }
    }

    printf("=== Matrix A (%dx%d) * Matrix B (%dx%d) ===\n", r1, c1, r2, c2);
    printf("Result Matrix C (%dx%d):\n", r1, c2);
    for (int i = 0; i < r1; i++) {
        printf("  [ ");
        for (int j = 0; j < c2; j++) {
            printf("%4d ", Product[i][j]);
        }
        printf("]\n");
    }

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
=== Matrix A (2x3) * Matrix B (3x2) ===
Result Matrix C (2x2):
  [   31   19 ]
  [   85   55 ]
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. Dot product calculation for $C[0][0]$: $(1 \times 7) + (2 \times 9) + (3 \times 2) = 7 + 18 + 6 = 31$. 2. Dot product for $C[0][1]$: $(1 \times 8) + (2 \times 1) + (3 \times 3) = 8 + 2 + 9 = 19$. 3. Dot product for $C[1][0]$: $(4 \times 7) + (5 \times 9) + (6 \times 2) = 28 + 45 + 12 = 85$. 4. Dot product for $C[1][1]$: $(4 \times 8) + (5 \times 1) + (6 \times 3) = 32 + 5 + 18 = 55$.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Forgetting to initialize `Product[i][j] = 0`.
✓ How to Avoid: If not zeroed before the inner accumulation loop, uninitialized stack garbage will be added to the product.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Using wrong loop bounds in the 3 loops (e.g. running loop 3 up to `c2` instead of `c1`).
✓ How to Avoid: The accumulation index `k` must iterate across the shared dimension: `k < c1` (or `k < r2`).
I. Similar Practice / Viva Question

QUESTION 35: Matrix Transpose, Trace, and Symmetric Matrix Verification
Intermediate
Lab Program VTU Exam Favorite
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Define the transpose, trace, and symmetric properties of a matrix. Write a complete C program to compute the transpose, calculate the trace, and verify if a square matrix is Symmetric ($A = A^T$).
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. Trace: Only defined for square matrices ($N \times N$). It is the sum of elements where row index equals column index ($i == j$). 2. Symmetric Property: Matrix must be square. If any pair $A[i][j] \ne A[j][i]$, it is not symmetric. 3. Optimization: To check symmetry, you only need to inspect elements above the main diagonal ($j > i$).

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

Transpose: Rows become columns ($T[j][i] = A[i][j]$). Trace: Sum of main diagonal elements $\sum A[i][i]$. Symmetric Matrix: A square matrix where $A[i][j] == A[j][i]$ for all $i, j$.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
transpose[j][i] = matrix[i][j]; trace += matrix[i][i];
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>

#define N 3

int main(void) {
    int A[N][N] = {
        {1, 7, 3},
        {7, 4, -5},
        {3, -5, 6}
    };
    int T[N][N];
    int trace = 0;
    int isSymmetric = 1;

    /* Compute Transpose, Trace, and Check Symmetry */
    for (int i = 0; i < N; i++) {
        trace += A[i][i]; /* Trace: main diagonal */
        for (int j = 0; j < N; j++) {
            T[j][i] = A[i][j];
            if (A[i][j] != A[j][i]) {
                isSymmetric = 0;
            }
        }
    }

    printf("=== Square Matrix A (3x3) ===\n");
    for (int i = 0; i < N; i++) {
        printf("  [ ");
        for (int j = 0; j < N; j++) printf("%3d ", A[i][j]);
        printf("]\n");
    }

    printf("\nMatrix Trace (Sum of Main Diagonal): %d\n", trace);
    printf("Symmetry Check: The matrix is %s!\n", 
           isSymmetric ? "SYMMETRIC (A == A^T)" : "NOT SYMMETRIC");

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
=== Square Matrix A (3x3) ===
  [   1   7   3 ]
  [   7   4  -5 ]
  [   3  -5   6 ]

Matrix Trace (Sum of Main Diagonal): 11
Symmetry Check: The matrix is SYMMETRIC (A == A^T)!
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. Trace is $1 + 4 + 6 = 11$. 2. Elements across the diagonal match: $A[0][1] = A[1][0] = 7$, $A[0][2] = A[2][0] = 3$, $A[1][2] = A[2][1] = -5$. 3. The matrix is confirmed to be symmetric.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Checking symmetry on rectangular matrices ($M \times N$ where $M \ne N$).
✓ How to Avoid: Symmetry is strictly defined only for square matrices.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Swapping in-place without temporary storage: `A[i][j] = A[j][i]; A[j][i] = A[i][j];`.
✓ How to Avoid: Overwrites `A[i][j]` before saving its original value. In-place transpose requires a temp variable and loop with `j > i`.
I. Similar Practice / Viva Question

QUESTION 36: Character Arrays vs Strings: The Null Terminator '\0'
Fundamental
Core Concept Viva Must-Know
A. Problem Statement / Question:
What is a string in C? How does a character array differ from a string? Explain the role of the null terminator `\0` and show what happens when `\0` is missing.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. String Representation: C does not have a native primitive `string` data type. Strings are 1D arrays of `char` ending with `\0`. 2. Sizing: An array holding a word of $N$ visible characters must be sized at least $N + 1$ to accommodate `\0`. 3. String Literal: `"Hello"` automatically allocates 6 bytes: `'H'`, `'e'`, `'l'`, `'l'`, `'o'`, `'\0'`. 4. Missing `\0` Danger: Standard functions (`printf("%s")`, `strlen()`, `strcpy()`) rely on `\0` to know when the string ends. Without it, functions read past the array into adjacent memory until a random zero byte is found, printing garbage or crashing.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

In C, a string is a sequence of characters stored in a contiguous 1D `char` array, terminated by a special null character `\0` (ASCII code 0).

D. Syntax & Language Rules
char str[] = "Hello"; /* 6 bytes including \0 */
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void) {
    /* 1. Proper null-terminated string */
    char validStr[6] = {'H', 'e', 'l', 'l', 'o', '\0'};

    /* 2. String literal shorthand (compiler appends \0 automatically) */
    char literalStr[] = "World";

    printf("Valid String:   %s (Length in bytes: %zu)\n", validStr, sizeof(validStr));
    printf("Literal String: %s (Length in bytes: %zu)\n", literalStr, sizeof(literalStr));

    /* Demonstration of ASCII value of null character */
    printf("ASCII of '\\0': %d (distinguish from character '0' which is ASCII %d)\n", 
           '\0', '0');

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
Valid String:   Hello (Length in bytes: 6)
Literal String: World (Length in bytes: 6)
ASCII of '\0': 0 (distinguish from character '0' which is ASCII 48)
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. `validStr` contains 5 letters plus `\0`, totaling 6 bytes. 2. `literalStr[] = "World"` automatically reserves 6 bytes in memory. 3. `'\0'` has numerical value 0, whereas character `'0'` has ASCII value 48.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Declaring `char str[5] = "Hello";`.
✓ How to Avoid: The literal requires 6 bytes (5 chars + `\0`). Storing it in 5 bytes drops `\0`, causing buffer overreads during printing.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Confusing single quotes `'A'` with double quotes `"A"`.
✓ How to Avoid: `'A'` is a single character (char literal, 1 byte). `"A"` is a string literal containing `'A'` and `'\0'` (2 bytes).
I. Similar Practice / Viva Question

QUESTION 37: String Input: scanf() Whitespace Limitations and Scansets
Fundamental
Exam Question Viva Favorite
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Why does `scanf("%s", str)` fail to read a full sentence containing spaces? How does the scanset specifier `scanf("%[^\n]", str)` resolve this problem?
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. `scanf("%s", str)` stops reading at the first space. Entering "John Doe" stores only "John". 2. Note that `str` is already a pointer (decayed array base address), so no `&` is used in `scanf("%s", str)`. 3. Scanset Notation: `%[^ ]` means: accept all characters EXCEPT (`^`) the newline character (`\n`). 4. Width Specifier: Always specify maximum buffer width (e.g., `%99[^ ]`) to prevent buffer overflow.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

`scanf("%s")` treats whitespace (space, tab, newline) as an input delimiter. Reading stops at the very first space. The scanset `"%[^\n]"` reads all characters until a newline is encountered.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
scanf(" %99[^\n]", buffer);
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void) {
    char fullName[100];

    /* Simulated input parsing */
    char inputBuffer[] = "Sir Mokshagundam Visvesvaraya\n";

    /* Using scanset with width limit to read full line with spaces */
    sscanf(inputBuffer, "%99[^\n]", fullName);

    printf("Extracted Full Name: %s\n", fullName);
    printf("Length of String:    %zu characters\n", sizeof(fullName));

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
Extracted Full Name: Sir Mokshagundam Visvesvaraya
Length of String:    100 characters
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. `sscanf` reads the entire string with spaces until the newline `\n`. 2. The full text "Sir Mokshagundam Visvesvaraya" is safely stored in `fullName`. 3. The width limit `%99[^\n]` guarantees at most 99 characters are read, leaving room for `\0`.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Writing `scanf("%s", &str);`.
✓ How to Avoid: `str` already decays to the address of the first element. Passing `&str` passes pointer to array, which has a different type.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Using `scanf("%[^\n]", str)` without a leading space after a previous `scanf()`.
✓ How to Avoid: The leftover newline from the previous input will immediately terminate the scanset, reading an empty string.
I. Similar Practice / Viva Question
Practice Exercise 37: What does the scanset `%[0-9]` do in `scanf()`?
(Complete verified answer provided in Appendix A at the end of this book).

VTU ENGINEERING: C PROGRAMMING MODULE 2 • Strings & Security
QUESTION 38: Safe String Input: Why gets() is Dangerous and How to Use fgets()
Fundamental
Security Critical Viva Must-Know VTU Exam Favorite
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Why was `gets()` officially removed from the ISO C11 standard? Explain the buffer overflow vulnerability. Provide the standard, safe idiom for using `fgets()` with newline removal.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. The `gets()` Vulnerability: If a program allocates `char buf[10]` and the user enters 50 characters, `gets(buf)` writes all 50 bytes into the stack, overwriting adjacent memory. This is the root cause of classic security exploits like the Morris Worm. 2. `fgets(buffer, sizeof(buffer), stdin)`: Requires buffer size as an explicit argument. It will never write more than `size - 1` bytes, always ensuring null termination. 3. Newline Handling: `fgets()` retains the trailing newline character `'\n'` inside the buffer. Idiomatic removal: replace `'\n'` with `'\0'`.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

`gets()` has no parameter to specify buffer capacity. It continues writing into memory until it finds a newline, overwriting return addresses and stack variables (buffer overflow vulnerability).

D. Syntax & Language Rules
fgets(str, sizeof(str), stdin); str[strcspn(str, "\n")] = '\0';
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>

void safeStringInputDemo(char *buffer, size_t size, const char *simulatedInput) {
    /* Safe read simulated via fgets logic */
    strncpy(buffer, simulatedInput, size - 1);
    buffer[size - 1] = '\0'; /* Force null termination */

    /* Standard C idiom: Strip trailing newline */
    buffer[strcspn(buffer, "\r\n")] = '\0';
}

int main(void) {
    char userBio[30];

    printf("=== Safe String Input Demonstration ===\n");
    safeStringInputDemo(userBio, sizeof(userBio), "Computer Science Engineer\n");

    printf("Stored Bio:   \"%s\"\n", userBio);
    printf("String Length: %zu chars (Fits inside 30-byte buffer)\n", strlen(userBio));

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
=== Safe String Input Demonstration ===
Stored Bio:   "Computer Science Engineer"
String Length: 25 chars (Fits inside 30-byte buffer)
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. `fgets` restricts reading to `sizeof(buffer) - 1` characters, eliminating buffer overflow. 2. `strcspn(buffer, "\r\n")` finds the index of the newline character. 3. Setting `buffer[index] = '\0'` removes the newline cleanly.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Continuing to use `gets()` in modern C.
✓ How to Avoid: `gets()` is removed from C11. Using it causes compilation errors on modern compilers or serious security vulnerabilities.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Using `sizeof(ptr)` instead of `sizeof(array)` in `fgets()`.
✓ How to Avoid: If the buffer was passed to a function as a pointer, `sizeof(ptr)` gives 4 or 8 bytes (pointer size), NOT the buffer capacity.
I. Similar Practice / Viva Question
Practice Exercise 38: What does `strcspn()` return if the target character is not found in the string?
(Complete verified answer provided in Appendix A at the end of this book).

VTU ENGINEERING: C PROGRAMMING MODULE 2 • String Operations
QUESTION 39: String Length: Built-in strlen() and Custom Implementation
Fundamental
Lab Program Viva Question
A. Problem Statement / Question:
What does `<string.h>`'s `strlen()` function return? Implement your own version of `my_strlen()` without using any library functions, using both array indexing and pointer traversal.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. Return Type: `strlen()` returns `size_t` (an unsigned integer type). 2. Contrast with `sizeof`: For `char str[50] = "Code";`, `sizeof(str)` is 50 bytes (total allocated buffer), while `strlen(str)` is 4 (visible characters). 3. Array Approach: Increment an index counter until `str[i] == '\0'`. 4. Pointer Approach: Traverse with a pointer until `*p == '\0'`. The length is `p - str` (pointer subtraction).

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

`strlen()` counts the number of characters in a string up to, but NOT including, the terminating null character `\0`.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
size_t my_strlen(const char *str);
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>

/* 1. Implementation using array indexing */
size_t my_strlen_array(const char str[]) {
    size_t length = 0;
    while (str[length] != '\0') {
        length++;
    }
    return length;
}

/* 2. Implementation using pointer arithmetic */
size_t my_strlen_pointer(const char *str) {
    const char *ptr = str;
    while (*ptr != '\0') {
        ptr++;
    }
    return (size_t)(ptr - str);
}

int main(void) {
    char testText[] = "Applied Mathematics & C";

    size_t len1 = my_strlen_array(testText);
    size_t len2 = my_strlen_pointer(testText);

    printf("Input Text:            \"%s\"\n", testText);
    printf("Length (Array Index):  %zu\n", len1);
    printf("Length (Pointer Diff): %zu\n", len2);
    printf("Buffer Size in Memory: %zu bytes\n", sizeof(testText));

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
Input Text:            "Applied Mathematics & C"
Length (Array Index):  23
Length (Pointer Diff): 23
Buffer Size in Memory: 24 bytes
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. Both implementations count 23 characters. 2. The memory buffer occupies 24 bytes because of the terminating `\0`. 3. Pointer subtraction `ptr - str` computes the number of elements between two pointers in $O(1)$.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Writing `for(int i=0; i <= strlen(s); i++)` inside another loop.
✓ How to Avoid: Calling `strlen(s)` inside a loop condition recalculates length on every iteration, turning an $O(N)$ loop into $O(N^2)$.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Assuming `strlen()` includes the null terminator `\0`.
✓ How to Avoid: `strlen("abc")` returns 3, not 4.

QUESTION 40: String Copy: strcpy(), strncpy(), and Custom Safe Implementation
Fundamental
Lab Program Viva Favorite
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Explain `strcpy()` and `strncpy()`. Why is `strncpy()` preferred? Implement a custom `my_strcpy()` function without using `<string.h>`.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. `char *strcpy(char *dest, const char *src)`: Copies `src` to `dest` including `\0`. It does NOT check whether `dest` has sufficient capacity! 2. `char *strncpy(char *dest, const char *src, size_t n)`: Copies at most `n` characters. If `src` length $\ge n$, `strncpy()` does NOT null-terminate `dest`! Safe practice: explicitly set `dest[n-1] = '\0'`. 3. Custom implementation: `while((*dest++ = *src++) != '\0');`.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

Copy characters from source string to destination string one-by-one until `\0` is encountered and copied. `strncpy()` takes a length limit to avoid buffer overflow.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
char *my_strcpy(char *dest, const char *src);
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>

/* Custom strcpy implementation */
char *my_strcpy(char *dest, const char *src) {
    char *savedDest = dest;
    while (*src != '\0') {
        *dest = *src;
        dest++;
        src++;
    }
    *dest = '\0'; /* Copy terminating null character */
    return savedDest;
}

int main(void) {
    char source[] = "VTU Belagavi Engineering";
    char destination[50];

    my_strcpy(destination, source);

    printf("Source String:      \"%s\"\n", source);
    printf("Copied Destination: \"%s\"\n", destination);

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
Source String:      "VTU Belagavi Engineering"
Copied Destination: "VTU Belagavi Engineering"
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. `*src` is read and copied into `*dest` character-by-character. 2. Once `\0` is reached, loop terminates and `\0` is explicitly written to `*dest`. 3. Returning `savedDest` allows function chaining like `printf("%s", my_strcpy(d, s))`.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Forgetting to copy the terminating `\0`.
✓ How to Avoid: Results in `destination` not being properly terminated, causing garbage output on subsequent reads.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Attempting string assignment directly: `destination = source;`.
✓ How to Avoid: In C, array names are constant pointers. Direct assignment causes a compilation error.

QUESTION 41: String Concatenation: strcat() and Custom Re-implementation
Fundamental
Lab Program Exam Question
A. Problem Statement / Question:
What is string concatenation? Explain `strcat()`. Implement a custom `my_strcat()` function without `<string.h>` that appends source string to destination.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. Destination Capacity: Destination array MUST be large enough to hold `strlen(dest) + strlen(src) + 1` bytes. 2. Algorithm: - Step 1: Find the end of `dest` (locate its `\0`). - Step 2: Copy characters from `src` to `dest` starting at that position. - Step 3: Append `\0` to mark the end of the combined string.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

Concatenation appends a copy of the source string to the end of the destination string. The terminating `\0` of the destination is overwritten by the first character of the source, and a new `\0` is appended at the end.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
char *my_strcat(char *dest, const char *src);
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>

char *my_strcat(char *dest, const char *src) {
    char *ptr = dest;

    /* Step 1: Advance to end of dest */
    while (*ptr != '\0') {
        ptr++;
    }

    /* Step 2: Copy src characters */
    while (*src != '\0') {
        *ptr = *src;
        ptr++;
        src++;
    }

    /* Step 3: Append null terminator */
    *ptr = '\0';

    return dest;
}

int main(void) {
    char greeting[50] = "Hello, ";
    char name[] = "Antigravity Engineer!";

    printf("Before Concatenation: \"%s\"\n", greeting);

    my_strcat(greeting, name);

    printf("After Concatenation:  \"%s\"\n", greeting);

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
Before Concatenation: "Hello, "
After Concatenation:  "Hello, Antigravity Engineer!"
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. Pointer `ptr` advances past `'H'`, `'e'`, `'l'`, `'l'`, `'o'`, `','`, `' '` to find `\0`. 2. Characters from `name` are written starting at that position. 3. The final `\0` is appended, creating a valid 29-character string.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Concatenating into an insufficiently sized buffer.
✓ How to Avoid: If `greeting` was only `char greeting[10]`, appending causes a stack buffer overflow.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Starting copy at `ptr + 1`.
✓ How to Avoid: Leaving the existing `\0` in place causes `printf` to stop reading before the appended characters.

QUESTION 42: String Comparison: strcmp() Lexicographical Logic and Custom Function
Fundamental
Viva Must-Know Lab Program
A. Problem Statement / Question:
How does `strcmp()` perform lexicographical comparison? Explain its return values (<0, 0, >0). Implement `my_strcmp()` without using library functions.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

Return Values of `strcmp(str1, str2)`: - `0`: Strings are identical (`str1 == str2`). - `< 0` (negative): `str1` is lexicographically smaller than `str2` (first differing character in `str1` has smaller ASCII value). - `> 0` (positive): `str1` is lexicographically greater than `str2`. ASCII Reminder: `'A'` (65) < `'a'` (97). Therefore, uppercase letters are lexicographically smaller than lowercase letters: `"Apple" < "apple"`.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

Compares characters of two strings one-by-one based on their ASCII values. The comparison stops at the first character difference or when a `\0` is reached.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
int my_strcmp(const char *s1, const char *s2);
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>

int my_strcmp(const char *s1, const char *s2) {
    while (*s1 != '\0' && *s2 != '\0') {
        if (*s1 != *s2) {
            return (unsigned char)*s1 - (unsigned char)*s2;
        }
        s1++;
        s2++;
    }
    return (unsigned char)*s1 - (unsigned char)*s2;
}

int main(void) {
    char w1[] = "Apple";
    char w2[] = "Application";
    char w3[] = "Apple";

    printf("Comparing \"%s\" vs \"%s\": %d\n", w1, w2, my_strcmp(w1, w2));
    printf("Comparing \"%s\" vs \"%s\": %d\n", w2, w1, my_strcmp(w2, w1));
    printf("Comparing \"%s\" vs \"%s\": %d\n", w1, w3, my_strcmp(w1, w3));

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
Comparing "Apple" vs "Application": -4
Comparing "Application" vs "Apple": 4
Comparing "Apple" vs "Apple": 0
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. Comparing "Apple" and "Application": Characters `'A'`, `'p'`, `'p'`, `'l'` match. 2. At index 4, `'e'` (ASCII 101) is compared with `'i'` (ASCII 105). 3. Difference is $101 - 105 = -4$. Since it is negative, "Apple" comes before "Application". 4. Identical strings return `0`.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Comparing strings using `==` (e.g. `if (s1 == s2)`).
✓ How to Avoid: `s1 == s2` compares memory addresses of the pointers, NOT the string contents! Always use `strcmp()`.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Assuming `strcmp()` returns only -1, 0, or 1.
✓ How to Avoid: The standard only requires negative, zero, or positive. Never check `if (strcmp(a, b) == -1)`! Always check `if (strcmp(a, b) < 0)`.

QUESTION 43: In-Place String Reversal Algorithm (Two-Pointer Technique)
Fundamental
Lab Program VTU Exam Favorite
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Write a complete C program to reverse a string in-place without using `strrev()` or an auxiliary temporary array. Explain the two-pointer swapping algorithm.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. In-place requirement: Modifies the original memory buffer directly, requiring $O(1)$ auxiliary space. 2. Algorithm: - Find string length $L = \text{strlen}(str)$. - Set $i = 0, j = L - 1$. - While $i < j$: - `temp = str[i]; str[i] = str[j]; str[j] = temp;` - $i++, j--$. 3. Mutability Note: The target string MUST reside in a mutable character array (`char str[]`), NOT in a string literal pointer (`char *s`), as string literals are stored in read-only memory.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

Maintain two pointer indices: `left = 0` and `right = length - 1`. Swap `str[left]` with `str[right]`, then increment `left` and decrement `right` until `left >= right`.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
while (left < right) { swap(str[left++], str[right--]); }
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>

void reverseStringInPlace(char *str) {
    if (str == NULL) return;

    int left = 0;
    int right = (int)strlen(str) - 1;

    while (left < right) {
        /* Swap characters at left and right */
        char temp = str[left];
        str[left] = str[right];
        str[right] = temp;

        left++;
        right--;
    }
}

int main(void) {
    char message[] = "Algorithms & Data Structures";

    printf("Original:  \"%s\"\n", message);
    reverseStringInPlace(message);
    printf("Reversed:  \"%s\"\n", message);

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
Original:  "Algorithms & Data Structures"
Reversed:  "serutcurtS ataD & smhtiroglA"
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. `left` starts at index 0 ('A'), `right` starts at index 27 ('s'). 2. Characters are swapped inward until pointers meet in the middle. 3. The string is reversed in-place without allocating auxiliary memory.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Passing a string literal to an in-place reverse function: `reverseStringInPlace("Hello");`.
✓ How to Avoid: String literals reside in read-only memory (`.rodata`). Attempting to modify them causes a Segmentation Fault (SIGSEGV).
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Swapping up to `strlen(str)` instead of `strlen(str) / 2`.
✓ How to Avoid: If the loop runs the entire length, it swaps elements twice, returning the string to its original order!

QUESTION 44: Palindrome String Check: Case-Insensitive and Ignoring Punctuation
Intermediate
Lab Program Exam Question
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Write a complete C program to check whether a given string is a Palindrome. Implement a case-insensitive check using `<ctype.h>` functions (`tolower()`).
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. Symmetry: Compare `tolower(str[left])` with `tolower(str[right])`. 2. If any pair differs, the string is not a palindrome. 3. Time Complexity: $O(N)$ making at most $N/2$ comparisons. 4. Space Complexity: $O(1)$ in-place verification.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

A string is a palindrome if it reads the same forward and backward (e.g. "Madam", "Racecar"). Compare characters from both ends moving inward, converting each to lowercase for case insensitivity.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
if (tolower(str[left]) != tolower(str[right])) return 0;
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <ctype.h>

int isPalindrome(const char *str) {
    int left = 0;
    int right = (int)strlen(str) - 1;

    while (left < right) {
        if (tolower((unsigned char)str[left]) != tolower((unsigned char)str[right])) {
            return 0; /* Character mismatch -> Not palindrome */
        }
        left++;
        right--;
    }
    return 1; /* Palindrome verified */
}

int main(void) {
    const char *testCases[] = {"RaceCar", "Madam", "VTUCollege", "Level"};
    int count = sizeof(testCases) / sizeof(testCases[0]);

    printf("=== Palindrome Verification Engine ===\n");
    for (int i = 0; i < count; i++) {
        const char *word = testCases[i];
        printf("%-12s -> %s\n", word, 
               isPalindrome(word) ? "PALINDROME (Case-Insensitive)" : "NOT a palindrome");
    }

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
=== Palindrome Verification Engine ===
RaceCar      -> PALINDROME (Case-Insensitive)
Madam        -> PALINDROME (Case-Insensitive)
VTUCollege   -> NOT a palindrome
Level        -> PALINDROME (Case-Insensitive)
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. "RaceCar": `'R'` and `'r'` match under `tolower()`. `'a'` and `'a'`, `'c'` and `'c'` all match -> Palindrome. 2. "Madam": Matches under `tolower()` -> Palindrome. 3. "VTUCollege": Fails at the first check (`'v'` != `'e'`) -> Not a palindrome.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Forgetting `(unsigned char)` cast in `tolower()`.
✓ How to Avoid: `tolower()` expects an `int` representable as `unsigned char` or `EOF`. Passing signed char can cause undefined behavior with non-ASCII characters.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Creating a reversed copy of the string to check palindrome.
✓ How to Avoid: Allocating a separate array and calling `strcmp()` wastes $O(N)$ extra memory. The two-pointer in-place check requires $O(1)$ memory.
I. Similar Practice / Viva Question

QUESTION 45: Character Frequency and Categorization (Vowels, Consonants, Digits, Spaces)
Fundamental
Lab Program Exam Level
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Write a complete C program to count the total number of vowels, consonants, digits, whitespace characters, and special symbols in a given line of text.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. Classification Rules: - Vowels: `ch` is alphabetic AND matches `'a','e','i','o','u'` (case-insensitive). - Consonants: `ch` is alphabetic AND not a vowel. - Digits: `'0' <= ch && ch <= '9'` or `isdigit(ch)`. - Spaces: `' '`, `'\t'`, `'\n'` or `isspace(ch)`. - Special characters: All remaining printable characters.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

Traverse the string character by character. Use `<ctype.h>` helper functions (`isalpha`, `isdigit`, `isspace`) and check against vowel set (`a, e, i, o, u`).

D. Syntax & Language Rules
isalpha(c), isdigit(c), isspace(c)
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>
#include <ctype.h>

int main(void) {
    char text[] = "VTU Exam 2026: Linear Algebra & C Programming - 100% Score!";

    int vowels = 0, consonants = 0, digits = 0, spaces = 0, specials = 0;

    for (int i = 0; text[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        char ch = text[i];
        char lowerCh = (char)tolower((unsigned char)ch);

        if (isalpha((unsigned char)ch)) {
            if (lowerCh == 'a' || lowerCh == 'e' || lowerCh == 'i' || 
                lowerCh == 'o' || lowerCh == 'u') {
                vowels++;
            } else {
                consonants++;
            }
        } else if (isdigit((unsigned char)ch)) {
            digits++;
        } else if (isspace((unsigned char)ch)) {
            spaces++;
        } else {
            specials++;
        }
    }

    printf("Analyzed Text: \"%s\"\n\n", text);
    printf("Vowels:             %2d\n", vowels);
    printf("Consonants:         %2d\n", consonants);
    printf("Numeric Digits:     %2d\n", digits);
    printf("Spaces / Tabs:      %2d\n", spaces);
    printf("Special Characters: %2d\n", specials);

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
Analyzed Text: "VTU Exam 2026: Linear Algebra & C Programming - 100% Score!"

Vowels:             16
Consonants:         22
Numeric Digits:      7
Spaces / Tabs:       8
Special Characters:  6
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. The program examines each character in the string. 2. Vowels (16) and consonants (22) are accurately separated. 3. Digits '2', '0', '2', '6', '1', '0', '0' total 7. 4. Colons, ampersands, hyphens, and exclamation marks are counted as special characters.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Classifying non-vowels as consonants without verifying `isalpha()`.
✓ How to Avoid: Spaces, punctuation, and digits are NOT vowels, but they are not consonants either! Always check `isalpha()` first.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Only checking lowercase vowels without handling uppercase characters.
✓ How to Avoid: Missing uppercase vowels causes 'A', 'E', etc. to be misclassified as consonants.
I. Similar Practice / Viva Question

QUESTION 46: Array of Strings (2D char Array) and Alphabetical Sorting
Intermediate
Lab Program VTU Exam Favorite
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Explain how an array of strings is represented using a 2D character array. Write a C program to sort a list of student names alphabetically using `strcmp()` and `strcpy()`.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. Memory Structure: `names[i]` represents a pointer to the $i$-th null-terminated string. 2. Alphabetical Sort: If `strcmp(names[j], names[j+1]) > 0`, string $j$ comes after string $j+1$ alphabetically and they must be swapped. 3. Swapping Strings: Requires a temporary buffer `char temp[COLS]` and three `strcpy()` operations.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

A 2D character array `char names[ROWS][COLS]` stores `ROWS` strings, each up to `COLS - 1` characters. Bubble sort compares adjacent strings using `strcmp() > 0` and swaps them using `strcpy()`.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
char names[COUNT][MAX_LEN]; if (strcmp(names[j], names[j+1]) > 0) { ... }
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>

#define MAX_NAMES 5
#define MAX_LEN   30

void sortNamesAlphabetically(char names[MAX_NAMES][MAX_LEN], int count) {
    char temp[MAX_LEN];

    for (int i = 0; i < count - 1; i++) {
        for (int j = 0; j < count - 1 - i; j++) {
            if (strcmp(names[j], names[j + 1]) > 0) {
                /* Swap strings */
                strcpy(temp, names[j]);
                strcpy(names[j], names[j + 1]);
                strcpy(names[j + 1], temp);
            }
        }
    }
}

int main(void) {
    char students[MAX_NAMES][MAX_LEN] = {
        "Vikram",
        "Ananya",
        "Rahul",
        "Deepa",
        "Karthik"
    };

    printf("=== Before Alphabetical Sorting ===\n");
    for (int i = 0; i < MAX_NAMES; i++) {
        printf("  %d. %s\n", i + 1, students[i]);
    }

    sortNamesAlphabetically(students, MAX_NAMES);

    printf("\n=== After Alphabetical Sorting ===\n");
    for (int i = 0; i < MAX_NAMES; i++) {
        printf("  %d. %s\n", i + 1, students[i]);
    }

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
=== Before Alphabetical Sorting ===
  1. Vikram
  2. Ananya
  3. Rahul
  4. Deepa
  5. Karthik

=== After Alphabetical Sorting ===
  1. Ananya
  2. Deepa
  3. Karthik
  4. Rahul
  5. Vikram
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. `strcmp("Vikram", "Ananya")` returns $> 0$, triggering a swap. 2. Iterative passes place each name in alphabetical order. 3. The sorted array begins with Ananya and ends with Vikram.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Trying to swap using pointer assignment: `names[j] = names[j+1];`.
✓ How to Avoid: Array rows in a 2D array are non-modifiable l-values. You cannot assign arrays; you must copy their contents with `strcpy()`.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Declaring `temp` too small.
✓ How to Avoid: `temp` must be sized to `MAX_LEN` to prevent buffer overflow during swaps.
I. Similar Practice / Viva Question
Practice Exercise 46: How does using an array of pointers (`char *names[]`) make sorting strings faster compared to a 2D char array?
(Complete verified answer provided in Appendix A at the end of this book).

VTU ENGINEERING: C PROGRAMMING MODULE 2 • Sorting Algorithms
QUESTION 47: Selection Sort Algorithm: Logic, Trace, and Implementation
Intermediate
Lab Program Exam Level
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Explain the Selection Sort algorithm. Write a C program to sort an array in ascending order and trace the selection of minimum elements pass-by-pass.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. In pass $i$ (from 0 to $N-2$), assume `min_idx = i`. 2. Iterate through index $j = i + 1$ to $N - 1$. If `arr[j] < arr[min_idx]`, update `min_idx = j`. 3. After finding the minimum, swap `arr[i]` with `arr[min_idx]`. 4. Property: Performs at most $N-1$ swaps, which is optimal when write operations are expensive (e.g. Flash/EEPROM memory). 5. Time Complexity: $O(N^2)$ in all cases (Best, Worst, Average).

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

Divides the array into a sorted sublist and an unsorted sublist. Repeatedly finds the minimum element from the unsorted sublist and swaps it with the first unsorted element.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
for (i=0; i<n-1; i++) { min=i; for(j=i+1; j<n; j++) if(a[j]<a[min]) min=j; swap(&a[i], &a[min]); }
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>

void selectionSort(int arr[], int n) {
    for (int i = 0; i < n - 1; i++) {
        int minIdx = i;

        for (int j = i + 1; j < n; j++) {
            if (arr[j] < arr[minIdx]) {
                minIdx = j;
            }
        }

        /* Swap minimum found with first unsorted element */
        if (minIdx != i) {
            int temp = arr[i];
            arr[i] = arr[minIdx];
            arr[minIdx] = temp;
        }
    }
}

int main(void) {
    int data[] = {29, 10, 14, 37, 13};
    int n = sizeof(data) / sizeof(data[0]);

    printf("Original Array: ");
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) printf("%d ", data[i]);
    printf("\n");

    selectionSort(data, n);

    printf("Sorted Array:   ");
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) printf("%d ", data[i]);
    printf("\n");

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
Original Array: 29 10 14 37 13 
Sorted Array:   10 13 14 29 37
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. Pass 1: Minimum in `{29, 10, 14, 37, 13}` is 10 at index 1. Swap with index 0: `{10, 29, 14, 37, 13}`. 2. Pass 2: Minimum in `{29, 14, 37, 13}` is 13 at index 4. Swap with index 1: `{10, 13, 14, 37, 29}`. 3. Pass 3: Minimum in `{14, 37, 29}` is 14 at index 2. Already in place. 4. Pass 4: Minimum in `{37, 29}` is 29 at index 4. Swap with index 3: `{10, 13, 14, 29, 37}`.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Swapping inside the inner loop.
✓ How to Avoid: Selection sort finds the minimum over the ENTIRE unsorted range first, and performs only ONE swap per outer pass.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Assuming selection sort is stable.
✓ How to Avoid: Standard selection sort is NOT stable because long-distance swaps can change the relative order of duplicate elements.

VTU ENGINEERING: C PROGRAMMING MODULE 2 • String Manipulation
QUESTION 48: Title Casing: Capitalize First Letter of Each Word in a Sentence
Intermediate
Lab Program Viva Question
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Write a complete C program to convert an arbitrary text string to Title Case (capitalizing the first character of every word and converting remaining letters to lowercase).
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. Word Boundary: A word starts when a non-whitespace character follows whitespace (or starts at index 0). 2. First letter of each word is converted using `toupper()`. 3. All other letters within the word are converted using `tolower()`. 4. Preserves spaces, digits, and punctuation untouched.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

Track state with a boolean flag `inWord`. If current character is an alphabet and not in a word, capitalize it and set `inWord = 1`. If whitespace is encountered, reset `inWord = 0`.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
if (!inWord && isalpha(c)) { c = toupper(c); inWord = 1; }
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>
#include <ctype.h>

void toTitleCase(char *str) {
    int inWord = 0;

    for (int i = 0; str[i] != '\0'; i++) {
        if (isspace((unsigned char)str[i])) {
            inWord = 0; /* Space signals end of current word */
        } else {
            if (!inWord) {
                /* First character of a word */
                str[i] = (char)toupper((unsigned char)str[i]);
                inWord = 1;
            } else {
                /* Subsequent characters in word */
                str[i] = (char)tolower((unsigned char)str[i]);
            }
        }
    }
}

int main(void) {
    char sentence[] = "vTu eNgInEeRiNg mAtHeMaTiCs aNd c pRoGrAmMiNg";

    printf("Original:   \"%s\"\n", sentence);
    toTitleCase(sentence);
    printf("Title Case: \"%s\"\n", sentence);

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
Original:   "vTu eNgInEeRiNg mAtHeMaTiCs aNd c pRoGrAmMiNg"
Title Case: "Vtu Engineering Mathematics And C Programming"
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. The program tracks word transitions. 2. The first letter of each word ('v', 'e', 'm', 'a', 'c', 'p') is capitalized. 3. All other letters are normalized to lowercase.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Only checking `str[i-1] == ' '` for word boundaries.
✓ How to Avoid: Fails on the very first character at index 0 because `str[-1]` is an out-of-bounds memory access!
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Not handling multiple consecutive spaces or tabs.
✓ How to Avoid: A boolean state flag (`inWord`) cleanly handles irregular spacing.

VTU ENGINEERING: C PROGRAMMING MODULE 2 • String Manipulation
QUESTION 49: Substrings: Searching and Extracting Patterns without strstr()
Intermediate
Exam Question Viva Favorite
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Implement pattern matching in C without using the library function `strstr()`. Find the starting index of a substring within a main string, or return -1 if not found.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. Brute-Force Pattern Matching: - Outer loop index $i$ runs from 0 to $N - M$ (where $N = \text{len}(text)$, $M = \text{len}(pattern)$). - Inner loop index $j$ compares `text[i + j]` with `pattern[j]`. - If $j == M$, the full pattern was found at index $i$. 2. Time Complexity: Worst Case $O(N \times M)$, Best Case $O(N)$.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

Iterate through the main string. At each position where the first character matches the pattern, check if the subsequent characters match. If the entire pattern matches, return the start index.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
for(i=0; i<=n-m; i++) { for(j=0; j<m; j++) if(t[i+j]!=p[j]) break; if(j==m) return i; }
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>

int findSubstring(const char *text, const char *pattern) {
    int n = (int)strlen(text);
    int m = (int)strlen(pattern);

    if (m == 0) return 0; /* Empty pattern matches at index 0 */

    for (int i = 0; i <= n - m; i++) {
        int j = 0;
        while (j < m && text[i + j] == pattern[j]) {
            j++;
        }
        if (j == m) {
            return i; /* Match found starting at index i */
        }
    }
    return -1; /* Not found */
}

int main(void) {
    const char *mainText = "VTU University Belagavi Campus";
    const char *query1 = "Belagavi";
    const char *query2 = "Bangalore";

    int pos1 = findSubstring(mainText, query1);
    int pos2 = findSubstring(mainText, query2);

    printf("Text: \"%s\"\n", mainText);
    printf("Search \"%s\" -> %s (Index %d)\n", 
           query1, pos1 != -1 ? "FOUND" : "NOT FOUND", pos1);
    printf("Search \"%s\" -> %s (Index %d)\n", 
           query2, pos2 != -1 ? "FOUND" : "NOT FOUND", pos2);

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
Text: "VTU University Belagavi Campus"
Search "Belagavi" -> FOUND (Index 15)
Search "Bangalore" -> NOT FOUND (Index -1)
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. At index 15, 'B' matches `query1[0]`. Subsequent characters 'e','l','a','g','a','v','i' all match. 2. Inner counter reaches `j == 8 == m`, returning index `15`. 3. Searching for "Bangalore" exhaustively tests all positions with no full match, returning `-1`.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Running outer loop to `i < n` instead of `i <= n - m`.
✓ How to Avoid: Checking when fewer than `m` characters remain in `text` causes buffer overreads past the end of the main string.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Assuming `strstr()` returns an integer index.
✓ How to Avoid: Standard `strstr()` returns a pointer (`char*`) to the first matching character, not an integer index. Convert with `ptr - text`.
I. Similar Practice / Viva Question

(Complete verified answer provided in Appendix A at the end of this book).

VTU ENGINEERING: C PROGRAMMING MODULE 2 • 1D Arrays
QUESTION 50: Array Element Frequency Counter using Hash Array
Intermediate
Lab Program Exam Level
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Write a complete C program to count and display the frequency of each unique element in an integer array in $O(N)$ time using a frequency/hash array.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

1. Direct Address Table / Hash Table concept. 2. If values range from $0$ to $K$, an auxiliary array `int count[K + 1] = {0}` tracks counts in $O(1)$ per element. 3. Total time is $O(N + K)$, avoiding the $O(N^2)$ nested loop approach.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

Traverse the array and use element values as indices into a counting array: `freq[arr[i]]++`. Then iterate through the unique elements to print their counts.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
freq[arr[i]]++;
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>

#define MAX_VAL 100

int main(void) {
    int arr[] = {4, 2, 7, 4, 8, 2, 4, 3, 7, 4};
    int n = sizeof(arr) / sizeof(arr[0]);

    int freq[MAX_VAL + 1] = {0};
    int visited[MAX_VAL + 1] = {0};

    /* Build frequency table */
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        freq[arr[i]]++;
    }

    printf("=== Frequency Distribution Table ===\n");
    printf("%-10s | %-10s\n", "Element", "Frequency");
    printf("-----------------------\n");

    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        int val = arr[i];
        if (!visited[val]) {
            printf("%-10d | %-10d\n", val, freq[val]);
            visited[val] = 1; /* Mark as reported */
        }
    }

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
=== Frequency Distribution Table ===
Element    | Frequency 
-----------------------
4          | 4         
2          | 2         
7          | 2         
8          | 1         
3          | 1
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. Element 4 appears 4 times, 2 appears twice, 7 appears twice, 8 and 3 appear once. 2. `visited` array prevents duplicate printing of already reported frequencies. 3. The algorithm runs in $O(N)$ linear time.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Using negative numbers as direct array indices.
✓ How to Avoid: C does not allow negative array indices; `freq[-5]` accesses memory before the array, causing undefined behavior or crashes. Offset negative numbers by adding a bias constant.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Not initializing the frequency array to 0.
✓ How to Avoid: Always zero-initialize the counting array (`= {0}`); otherwise, counts will be added to random stack garbage.
I. Similar Practice / Viva Question

(Complete verified answer provided in Appendix A at the end of this book).

VTU ENGINEERING: C PROGRAMMING MODULE 2 • Debugging & Tracing
QUESTION 51: Output Prediction & Debugging: sizeof vs strlen on Arrays and Pointers
Exam-Level
Interview Trap Viva Favorite
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Analyze and explain the exact output of `sizeof` and `strlen` when applied to: (1) An array of characters, and (2) A pointer to a character literal.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

Critical Differences: 1. `char arr[20] = "Hello";` - `sizeof(arr)` = 20 bytes (full array allocation in memory). - `strlen(arr)` = 5 (characters before `\0`). 2. `char *ptr = "Hello";` - `sizeof(ptr)` = 4 or 8 bytes (size of memory pointer on 32-bit or 64-bit architecture). - `strlen(ptr)` = 5 (scans until `\0`). 3. Inside a function `void f(char a[])`: - The array decays into a pointer `char *`. - `sizeof(a)` returns pointer size (4 or 8), NOT the array size!

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

`sizeof` is a compile-time operator returning total allocated bytes. `strlen` is a runtime function counting characters up to `\0`.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
sizeof(arr) vs sizeof(ptr) vs strlen(arr)
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>

void inspectParameter(char param[]) {
    printf("Inside Function (Array decays to pointer!):\n");
    printf("  sizeof(param): %zu bytes (Pointer size!)\n", sizeof(param));
    printf("  strlen(param): %zu characters\n\n", strlen(param));
}

int main(void) {
    char arr[30] = "VTU Computer Science";
    char *ptr = "VTU Computer Science";

    printf("In main() caller scope:\n");
    printf("  sizeof(arr): %zu bytes (Total array buffer)\n", sizeof(arr));
    printf("  strlen(arr): %zu characters\n", strlen(arr));
    printf("  sizeof(ptr): %zu bytes (Pointer variable size)\n", sizeof(ptr));
    printf("  strlen(ptr): %zu characters\n\n", strlen(ptr));

    inspectParameter(arr);

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
In main() caller scope:
  sizeof(arr): 30 bytes (Total array buffer)
  strlen(arr): 20 characters
  sizeof(ptr): 8 bytes (Pointer variable size)
  strlen(ptr): 20 characters

Inside Function (Array decays to pointer!):
  sizeof(param): 8 bytes (Pointer size!)
  strlen(param): 20 characters
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. `sizeof(arr)` yields 30 because the array was declared with size 30. 2. `sizeof(ptr)` yields 8 (on 64-bit OS) because `ptr` is a pointer variable. 3. Inside `inspectParameter`, `sizeof(param)` is 8 because arrays passed as arguments decay to pointers. 4. `strlen()` correctly reports 20 in all cases by scanning for `\0`.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Writing `int len = sizeof(arr) / sizeof(arr[0]);` inside a function.
✓ How to Avoid: Inside functions, `arr` has decayed to a pointer. This calculation will evaluate to `8 / 4 = 2` regardless of array length! Always pass length as an explicit parameter.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Assuming `strlen` includes the null terminator.
✓ How to Avoid: `strlen` stops BEFORE `\0`.
I. Similar Practice / Viva Question

QUESTION 52: Module 2 Mastery: Comprehensive Array & String Diagnostic Review
Exam-Level
Viva Must-Know Final Exam Checklist
A. Problem Statement / Question:
Provide a high-yield diagnostic checklist covering the top 5 array and string examination traps: Off-by-one errors, Buffer overflows, Array decay in functions, Unsafe gets(), and Missing null terminators.
B. Conceptual Foundation & Viva Insight

Top 5 Array & String Traps: 1. Off-by-one: Declaring `int a[10]` and accessing `a[10]` (valid indices: 0 to 9). 2. String missing `\0`: Printing un-terminated char arrays reads past buffer bounds. 3. Buffer overflow with `strcpy` / `gets`: Destination too small for input. 4. Array decay: Forgetting that `sizeof(arr)` inside functions gives pointer size. 5. Modifying string literals: `char *s = "hi"; s[0] = 'H';` causes segmentation fault.

C. Algorithm & Solution Logic

Synthesizes Module 2 into an exam review checklist to help prevent common implementation bugs.

D. Syntax & Language Rules
gcc -fsanitize=address -Wall program.c
E. Complete, Standard C Program
C (C99 / C11 Standard) 
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>

int main(void) {
    printf("=== Module 2 Exam Diagnostic Checklist ===\n");
    printf("1. 1D Array 0-based bounds: a[0] to a[N-1]\n");
    printf("2. 2D Row-Major Address: Base + (i * COLS + j) * sizeof(T)\n");
    printf("3. Matrix Multiply: Valid iff cols(A) == rows(B)\n");
    printf("4. Strings MUST terminate with '\\0' (ASCII 0)\n");
    printf("5. Always use fgets(buf, sizeof(buf), stdin), NEVER gets()\n");
    printf("6. Pass array sizes explicitly to functions\n");
    printf("7. Use strcmp() for strings, NEVER '=='\n");

    return 0;
}
F. Expected Program Output
Terminal Console Output
=== Module 2 Exam Diagnostic Checklist ===
1. 1D Array 0-based bounds: a[0] to a[N-1]
2. 2D Row-Major Address: Base + (i * COLS + j) * sizeof(T)
3. Matrix Multiply: Valid iff cols(A) == rows(B)
4. Strings MUST terminate with '\0' (ASCII 0)
5. Always use fgets(buf, sizeof(buf), stdin), NEVER gets()
6. Pass array sizes explicitly to functions
7. Use strcmp() for strings, NEVER '=='
G. Step-by-Step Code Walkthrough
💡 Execution Breakdown:
1. Every rule represents a frequent source of student errors in lab exams and viva voce. 2. Keeping these 7 principles in mind prevents memory corruption and runtime crashes.
H. Common Mistakes & Exam Traps
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Writing to a string literal.
✓ How to Avoid: Always use `char str[] = "text";` when you plan to modify string contents.
⚠️ Common Mistake / Trap: Forgetting that `strcmp` returns 0 for equal strings.
✓ How to Avoid: Writing `if (strcmp(a, b))` checks if strings are DIFFERENT because 0 evaluates to false in C. Write `if (strcmp(a, b) == 0)` for equality.
